## A.18 Co-financing Details
### A.18.1 Source of funds for the Environment (SFE)
### A.18.2 Source of funds for the Environment (SFE)

The project is a joint effort between [Agency Name] and [Partner Agency], with funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and a co-financing partner. The project aims to enhance climate resilience in vulnerable communities by implementing sustainable land management practices, improving water governance, and promoting ecosystem restoration.

### A.18.3 Co-benefits
The project will contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including:
- **No Poverty** (SDG 1): By enhancing livelihoods and food security.
- **Zero Hunger** (SDG 2): Through improved agricultural practices.
- **Good Health and Well-being** (SDG 3): By reducing health risks associated with climate change.
- **Quality Education** (SDG 4): By providing training and education on sustainable land management.
- **Gender Equality** (SDG 5): By empowering women and promoting gender equality.
- **Clean Water and Sanitation** (SDG 6): Through improved water governance.
- **Affordable and Clean Energy** (SDG 7): By promoting renewable energy sources.

### A.18.4 Climate Change Mitigation
The project will contribute to climate change mitigation by:
- Reducing greenhouse gas emissions through the adoption of sustainable land management practices.
- Enhancing carbon sequestration in soils and vegetation.
- Promoting the use of renewable energy sources.

### A.18.5 Climate Change Adaptation
The project will enhance climate change adaptation by:
- Improving water governance to ensure access to clean water for vulnerable communities.
- Implementing sustainable land management practices that increase resilience to climate-related impacts.
- Promoting ecosystem restoration to enhance biodiversity and improve ecosystem services.

### A.18.6 Health, Gender, and Social Safeguards
The project will include health, gender, and social safeguards to ensure the well-being of all beneficiaries. This includes:
- Ensuring equal access to project benefits for men and women.
- Promoting gender equality and empowering women.
- Implementing health and safety measures to protect workers and communities.

### A.18.7 Risk Management
The project will include a comprehensive risk management plan to address potential challenges, including:
- Climate-related risks such as droughts, floods, and extreme weather events.
- Financial risks associated with the implementation of the project.
- Operational risks related to the delivery of project activities.

30-30 Foundation and friends of Earthworks' vision aims to create a global coalition for climate justice and environmental protection. The foundation is committed to supporting the work of Henry Bobbitt, a National Laboratory and medical engineer, who has led to the development of new technologies that have helped to reduce the impact of COVID-19 on communities around the world.

The foundation's mission is to support the development of innovative solutions for climate change and environmental protection. The foundation's vision is to create a global coalition of individuals, organizations, and governments working together to address the challenges of climate change and environmental degradation.

The foundation's goals are to:

- Support the development of innovative technologies that can help to reduce the impact of climate change on communities around the world.
- Support the development of policies and programs that can help to mitigate the impacts of climate change on communities around the world.
- Support the development of educational programs that can help to raise awareness about the challenges of climate change and environmental protection.

The foundation's objectives are to:

1. Strengthen delivery models and legislations for climate information and multi-sector early warning systems;
2. Enhance observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts;
3. Improve understanding of climate risks and vulnerabilities in key sectors such as health, water, agriculture, energy, transport, disaster risk management and urban planning.

## 4. Enhanced climate risk management capacity

The Project will enable planning and early action for climate resilience through enhanced climate risk management (ENRM) and Forest-based Financing (FF). The Project will build the capacities of three Leads to implement ENRM and FF, including the development of a climate risk management framework and the development of a forest-based financing strategy. The Project will also support the development of an early warning system for climate risks and provide training on climate risk management and adaptation planning. The Project will also support the development of a climate risk management plan that includes a risk assessment and a risk management strategy.

The Project will strengthen implementation of the NMO Climate Change Framework for Climate Services (CCFS) through the following activities:

1. Early Warning System: Implement an Early Warning System (EWS) to provide timely and accurate information on climate risks.
2. Climate Risk Management: Develop and implement a climate risk management plan that includes a risk assessment, risk management strategy, and a monitoring and evaluation framework.
3. Forest-based Financing: Develop and implement a forest-based financing strategy that includes a financial analysis of the costs and benefits of forest-based financing.

The Project will also support the development of an Early Warning System (EWS) for climate risks through the following activities:

1. Early Warning System: Implement an Early Warning System (EWS) to provide timely and accurate information on climate risks.
2. Climate Risk Management: Develop and implement a climate risk management plan that includes a risk assessment, risk management strategy, and a monitoring and evaluation framework.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAM INFORMATION

## S.1 Climate vulnerability and impacts (last 30 years, approximately 2 pages)

### Background

The Least Developed Countries (LDC) and Small Island Developing States (SIDS), including the Republic of the Marshall Islands, are on the frontline of climate change. The Marshall Islands is located in a region that is vulnerable to sea-level rise and extreme weather events. The country is also exposed to other environmental challenges such as ocean acidification and coral bleaching.

Timothy Lei has a master's degree, which is focused on the impacts of climate change on the Marshall Islands. He is currently working for the Department of Environment and Emergency Management (DEEM) in Majuro, the capital city of the Marshall Islands. The DEEM is responsible for managing environmental issues such as waste management, water quality, and air pollution.

### Historical climate trends and expected future climate change

In the last 30 years, temperatures in the Marshall Islands have increased by 1.5 - 2.8°C. Data from 1979-2005 suggest that the average increase is 0.1 - 0.3°C per decade. There is an increasing trend of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, which are expected to continue in the future.

The Marshall Islands have experienced a significant increase in sea surface temperatures (SST) over the past few decades. The SST has increased by approximately 1.5 - 2°C since 1970. This is consistent with global trends of increasing SSTs and ocean warming.

### References

- Brown, J., et al. (2007). Vulnerability to climate change and disaster risk in the Pacific Islands.
- IPCC (2007). Climate Change 2007: Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability. Cambridge University Press.
- Lei, T. (2018). The impact of climate change on the Marshall Islands. Unpublished manuscript.
- Marshall Islands Government (2019). National Adaptation Plan for the Republic of the Marshall Islands.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2 | PAGE OF 118

## Key climate hazards related to increasing temperatures are outlined below

- **Heatwaves** are projected to increase in frequency. "Much extreme temperature could result in increased heat-related mortality and morbidity, particularly among the elderly, children, and the infirm. The incidence of vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever is also likely to increase."

- **Wildfires** have been linked to a classification as high in amount of Tier-List, meaning that there is more than 50% chance of encountering weather hot enough to support significant wildfire that is likely to result in both human and environmental damage. "The risk of wildfire is expected to increase significantly over the coming decades, particularly in areas with highly vulnerable ecosystems. Rapid warming and wetter landscapes are a concerning trend for fire-prone regions such as Australia and the western United States. Wildfires have been linked to increased frequency and severity of weather events." This is an outcome of increasing temperatures with more hot days and longer fire seasons. "Wildfire is a major source of fine particulate matter (PM2.5), which is a significant concern for public health."

- **Increased air pollution:** Air quality is strongly dependent on weather and is therefore sensitive to climate change. Increasing temperatures and changes in precipitation are likely to increase the concentration of the following pollutants: ground-level ozone, nitrogen dioxide, sulfur dioxide, carbon monoxide, and particulate matter. "Air quality is expected to decrease by 1-3% per decade over the coming decades." Increased air pollution has significant impacts on human health and increases the risk of mortality and severity of allergies.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v.2 | PAGE 1 OF 11

## Ranfail

In the period 1981-1996, mean annual rainfall in Timor-Leste decreased relative to 1931-1960. Long-term trends indicate a significant decrease in rainfall over the period 1975-2005. Long-term drought trends in central areas have been observed during the last decade and are expected to continue into the future. These changes are indicative of climate change.

Drought is a natural disaster and associated with an increase of desertification that has occurred in Timor-Leste over the past 30 years. The mean annual rainfall has decreased by about 25% from the current climate pattern. The drought period is expected to continue for at least another decade, which may lead to a decrease in the total area of arable water regions (beaches) and coastal areas. This will have an impact on the livelihoods of local communities who depend on agriculture, particularly smallholder farmers.

In addition, sea-level rise may be accelerated by about 30 years from the current climate pattern. The drought period is expected to continue for at least another decade, which may lead to a decrease in the total area of arable water regions (beaches) and coastal areas. This will have an impact on the livelihoods of local communities who depend on agriculture, particularly smallholder farmers.

Floods are one of the most frequently occurring extreme events in Timor-Leste, resulting from a combination of heavy rainfall and high-intensity storms. The most affected districts include Ermera, Bobonaro, and Liquica. Flooding is expected to increase with climate change, particularly during the wet season. The frequency and intensity of floods are expected to increase due to changes in precipitation patterns.

Landslides induced by flooding are reported to be one of the most common disasters in Timor-Leste, with an average of 10 landslide events per year over the past decade. Landslides have been observed in high-risk areas such as steep slopes, high weathering rates and brittle materials with low shear resistance.

## References

- State of the Environment Report - Timor-Leste, 2017.
- Climate Change Knowledge Portal - World Bank, 2018.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v.2 | PAGE OF 118

## B

Drought/Timor-Leste experiences periodic extreme drought events associated with El Niño events. The impacts of these droughts are exacerbated by poor water resource management under RCP8.5 and other scenarios, which project there to be two to three times more drought events in the future compared to historical records. These droughts have devastating impacts on agriculture productivity and rural livelihoods. Therefore, it is critical that the Government of Timor-Leste takes steps to address these issues.

### Tropical cyclones and storms

Since tropical cyclones developed within or passed through the Timor-Leste EEZ in the period 1980 - 2010, at least one tropical cyclone has affected Timor-Leste each year. The frequency of tropical cyclones is expected to increase by about 5% per decade with an increase in intensity and rainfall of around 20%, with 10% of the storms coming within the 50km radius of Dili. Increased sea surface temperatures are likely to lead to increased size and damage, increased frequency of extreme storm events, loss of coastal habitats and increased risk of flooding.

### Sea level rise

Sea level has risen near Timor-Leste by about 3mm per year since 1993. This rate is two to three times faster than the global average. The IPCC projects that sea levels will continue to rise at a rate of between approximately 18 cm by 2050, with increases of 20-60 cm possible under higher emission scenarios. Increased sea level rise will lead to increased coastal erosion and flooding.

### Ocean acidification and deoxygenation

Ocean acidification is becoming a major threat to Timor-Leste's waters and is projected to continue to increase through the 21st century. Ocean acidification, along with deoxygenation, reduces its natural abundance, carbon stocks, conservation, in particular, aquaculture, coral reefs, and other marine resources. Deoxygenation of sea water is a serious threat to many species, including tuna, mackerel, snapper, and other marketable fish. Ocean acidification and deoxygenation reduce the availability of fish for inland communities.

* IPCC (2014) Climate Change: Impacts and Adaptation. Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change.
* IPCC (2018) Special Report: Global Warming of 1.5°C. Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change.

# Vulnerability of Timor-Leste to climate hazards and climate change

Timor-Leste is highly vulnerable to climate change and climate-induced hazards, and experiences recurrent natural disasters. The vulnerability is due to a combination of factors such as high population vulnerability and weak governance, low adaptive capacity, and limited resources for disaster risk reduction. Climate change impacts are expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, including floods, droughts, and storms. These impacts will have significant implications for the country's development.

About 70% of Timor-Leste's population live in rural areas and rely on agriculture as their primary source of income. The agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate change, with a lack of access to modern technologies and limited capacity to adapt to changing climatic conditions. The vulnerability is exacerbated by weak infrastructure and low levels of education and awareness about climate change.

Climate change presents significant challenges for Timor-Leste's development goals, including poverty reduction, food security, and health outcomes. The impacts of climate change will have profound implications for the country's ability to achieve its Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) and other development objectives. The government has recognized the need to address climate change as a priority and has developed a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to identify and implement adaptation measures.

The majority of climate-related disasters in Timor-Leste are associated with periods of high rainfall, which results in significant flooding and landslides. In 2016, heavy rains caused widespread flooding and landslides that affected more than 43,000 people and damaged or destroyed over 500 homes. The impacts of climate change on agriculture and food security are also expected to increase the vulnerability of communities to natural disasters.

The government has developed a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to identify and implement adaptation measures to reduce the impact of climate change on the country's development goals. The NAPA focuses on reducing the risk of natural disasters, improving water resources management, and enhancing the resilience of vulnerable communities.

The vulnerability of Timor-Leste to climate hazards is expected to increase in the coming years as the impacts of climate change become more pronounced. The government has recognized the need to address this challenge and has developed a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to identify and implement adaptation measures to reduce the impact of climate change on the country's development goals.

# Climate Change Priorities

To accelerate economic health outcomes (e.g., deaths from extreme weather events, food security, water security), the following priorities are recommended:

- Improve early warning systems for extreme weather events.
- Enhance disaster risk management and response capabilities.
- Strengthen climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.
- Promote sustainable agriculture and forestry practices.

# Climate Change Initiatives

The government has developed a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to identify and implement adaptation measures to reduce the impact of climate change on the country's development goals. The NAPA focuses on reducing the risk of natural disasters, improving water resources management, and enhancing the resilience of vulnerable communities.

### Vulnerability due to COVID-19 and interconnection with climate change

As of 6 May 2021 COVID-19 has caused over 3.4 million deaths worldwide, with over 170 million confirmed cases. The World Health Organization (WHO) declared the COVID-19 pandemic a global emergency on 30 January 2020 and a global pandemic on 11 March 2020. The pandemic has had significant impacts on health systems, economies and societies worldwide. The COVID-19 pandemic has highlighted the importance of strong health systems to respond effectively to public health threats. The pandemic has also exposed the vulnerability of health systems to external shocks, such as the ongoing economic impact of the pandemic, which could be exacerbated by climate change. The pandemic has also highlighted the need for a One Health approach that recognizes the interconnection between people, animals and their shared environment.

The COVID-19 pandemic has had significant impacts on health systems worldwide, causing disruptions to health services, increased demand for hospital beds and intensive care units, and increased mortality rates. The pandemic has also highlighted the importance of strong health systems to respond effectively to public health threats. The pandemic has also exposed the vulnerability of health systems to external shocks, such as the ongoing economic impact of the pandemic, which could be exacerbated by climate change.

The COVID-19 pandemic has had significant impacts on economies worldwide, causing disruptions to supply chains, increased unemployment and reduced economic growth. The pandemic has also highlighted the importance of strong economic policies to respond effectively to economic shocks. The pandemic has also exposed the vulnerability of economies to external shocks, such as the ongoing economic impact of the pandemic, which could be exacerbated by climate change.

The COVID-19 pandemic has had significant impacts on societies worldwide, causing disruptions to social services, increased inequality and reduced social cohesion. The pandemic has also highlighted the importance of strong social policies to respond effectively to social shocks. The pandemic has also exposed the vulnerability of societies to external shocks, such as the ongoing economic impact of the pandemic, which could be exacerbated by climate change.

The data are processed using various software patterns, and can be obtained by the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) for analysis and forecasting purposes. The results of the station and their condition are regularly updated. The recent survey focusing on the hydrometeorological observation network indicates that the network is in good condition.

- There is an end-to-end early warning system (EWIS) in place at Time-Lapse and classical risk management is implemented. Real-time EWIS are achieved in Free-Level average temperature and rainfall (DMRG) stations working on the same system as the NMHS. The NMHS has a responsibility for issuing warnings and forecasts.

### Current status of disaster risk management

In 2019, the State of the Global Climate report observed DMRG. The National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) was established to coordinate disaster response efforts. The NDMA has a mandate to develop and implement policies and programs for disaster preparedness, mitigation, and response. The NDMA is also responsible for providing technical assistance and training to local governments and communities.

The NDMA has developed a comprehensive framework for disaster risk management that includes the following components:

- **Risk assessment**: The NDMA conducts regular risk assessments to identify areas at high risk of disasters.
- **Early warning systems**: The NDMA operates early warning systems to provide timely information to communities at risk.
- **Preparedness and response planning**: The NDMA develops preparedness and response plans for different types of disasters.
- **Capacity building**: The NDMA provides training and capacity-building programs for local governments, communities, and other stakeholders.

The NDMA has also established partnerships with international organizations and other countries to enhance its disaster risk management capabilities. These partnerships provide access to resources, expertise, and technology that can help improve the effectiveness of the NDMA's efforts.

The National Disaster Management Directorate (NDMD) is responsible for providing disaster risk management services and coordinating disaster response activities in Lesotho. The NDMD is composed of the National Disaster Coordination Centre (NDCC) at its headquarters in Maseru, and six Regional Disaster Coordination Committees (RDCCs). The NDMD is also supported by the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA).

## List of negative, regulatory and policy barriers for climate finance and disaster risk

i) Inadequate observation systems and sector-specific climate information (e.g., health, agriculture, disaster risk reduction), environmental management;

ii) Lack of reliable climate risk information and data collection systems;

iii) Limited capacity and funding to prepare for and manage climate risks.

### Problem Statement

The national economy and most people’s livelihoods depend on climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture, fisheries, forestry, tourism, and mining. These sectors are vulnerable to the impacts of natural hazards, agricultural pests and diseases, urban floods, and other disasters. Farmers are often forced from the land due to drought or crop failure; coastal communities suffer from sea-level rise, floods, tsunamis, storms, and infrastructure damage by extreme weather events. The health sector is also affected by climate change, which poses further risks to both human and environmental health.

The impact of climate change on the economy and people’s livelihoods is exacerbated by weak governance, lack of access to finance, and inadequate risk management capacity. This has led to a lack of investment in adaptation measures and resilience-building initiatives. The lack of reliable data and information on climate impacts and risks hinders effective planning and implementation of adaptation strategies.

### The role of the DNMOG

The staff of the DNMOG are constrained by a lack of funds, financial and technical resources in their capacity to provide adequate support to the government. There is no mechanism for value chain financing to deliver services and products to farmers and other users. The DNMOG has limited access to data collection tools and systems that can be used to collect and analyze climate information. This limits the ability of the DNMOG to provide timely, accurate, and reliable information on climate risks.

### Without the Proposed Project, the DNMOG will continue to provide basic weather forecasts with the help of external support from international organizations. The lack of funds will not enable them to reach their intended audiences in a timely manner. The cost of appliances such as computers and other equipment is too high for the DNMOG to afford. Data collection and analysis planning, early action on public health hazards, and disaster risk management systems have been identified by the government as areas that need to be strengthened. The lack of funds has limited the capacity of the DNMOG to provide adequate support to the government.

### The proposed project will address these challenges by:

- Providing financial resources for climate information services;
- Strengthening the capacity of the DNMOG to deliver climate information and early warning systems;
- Improving data collection, analysis, and dissemination of climate information;
- Enhancing the ability of the DNMOG to provide timely and accurate climate information.

### Expected achievements in reducing vulnerability

The proposed project will support the government’s efforts to reduce disaster risk by:

- Strengthening the capacity of the DNMOG to deliver climate information services;
- Improving data collection, analysis, and dissemination of climate information;
- Enhancing the ability of the DNMOG to provide timely and accurate climate information.

### Expected achievements in reducing vulnerability

The proposed project will support the government’s efforts to reduce disaster risk by:

- Strengthening the capacity of the DNMOG to deliver climate information services;
- Improving data collection, analysis, and dissemination of climate information;
- Enhancing the ability of the DNMOG to provide timely and accurate climate information.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### Objectives and Foreseen Results

This will result in a framework to increase resilience to climate change impacts by reducing vulnerability of communities to extreme weather events through improved access to climate information services.

- **Result 1** will provide an enabling environment for adaptation and reduced vulnerability by developing and implementing a national framework and policies for climate information services. This includes enhancing the capacity of meteorological services, improving data collection and dissemination, and ensuring that climate information is accessible to all users.
- **Result 2** will enhance the use of climate information services by providing early warning systems for extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and cyclones. This includes developing and disseminating early warning systems and enhancing community preparedness through public awareness campaigns.
- **Result 3** will address the need to enhance communication and dissemination of climate hazards and risk management strategies to vulnerable populations. This includes developing targeted communication strategies tailored to different vulnerabilities of groups, populations, and communities. The project will help improve a socially inclusive approach to climate change adaptation by enhancing community participation in decision-making processes.
- **Result 4** will assess the need for increased investment in climate-smart agriculture practices that enhance productivity and resilience to climate change. This includes developing and implementing climate-smart agricultural practices and enhancing the capacity of farmers to adopt these practices.

The proposed interventions are designed to address the key barriers to delivering sustainable climate services and increasing access to climate information by vulnerable communities. The project will create an enabling environment for long-term climate resilience, which in turn produces climate benefits. The Project will also contribute to the implementation of the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), with a focus on improving the lives of people affected by climate change.

The project will be implemented through a combination of national, regional, and local activities, with a strong emphasis on community participation and engagement. The project will be aligned with the objectives of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Paris Agreement, and will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

The proposed Project will develop integrated climate information services covering climate and impact based early warning systems to improve the resilience of communities and livelihoods in Africa.

## Objectives

1. To strengthen the integration of climate information into national and regional early warning systems.
2. To enhance the use of climate information for disaster risk management, including drought and flood preparedness and response.
3. To support the development of climate services for agriculture, water resources, health, and other sectors.

## Results

### Result 1: Strengthened delivery model and institutionalization for climate information and multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS)

This result will enable Timb-Lite to establish a National Framework by Climate Services (NFCS), which will be used to integrate climate services into national MHEWS. The NFCS will:

- Support the integration of climate information into national MHEWS.
- Establish the integration of disaster risk management and climate services into national policies, strategies, and plans.
- Enhance the integration of disaster risk management and climate services into national policies, strategies, and plans.

### Result 2: Enhanced use of climate information for disaster risk management

This result will enhance the use of climate information for disaster risk management. The enhanced use of climate information will:

- Improve the resilience of communities to climate-related disasters.
- Enhance the integration of climate information into national policies, strategies, and plans.

### Result 3: Enhanced sustainability of climate services and disaster risk management

This result will enhance the sustainability of climate services and disaster risk management. The enhanced sustainability of climate services and disaster risk management will:

- Improve the resilience of communities to climate-related disasters.
- Enhance the integration of climate information into national policies, strategies, and plans.

## Implementation

The project will be implemented through a series of activities that include:

- Development of a National Framework by Climate Services (NFCS).
- Establishment of a National Framework for Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS).
- Integration of climate information into national MHEWS.
- Enhancement of the integration of disaster risk management and climate services into national policies, strategies, and plans.

## Funding

The project is funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) through its Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme. The total funding for the project is USD 2 million, with a co-financing requirement of USD 500,000 from the implementing entity.

#### Subsection 1.1.1: Establish a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS)

The Proposal will develop National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to strengthen the integration and provision of climate services in national planning processes. The NFCS will be developed through:

- **Capacity Building**: Enhancing the capacity of stakeholders to effectively use climate information.
- **Stakeholder Engagement**: Engaging with key stakeholders, including policymakers, practitioners, and communities.
- **Policy Development**: Developing national policies that support the integration of climate services into decision-making processes.

- A quantum leap in institutional coordination and cooperation among relevant technical bodies and institutions at global, regional and national levels is required to address climate change challenges and opportunities. This will enable sector agencies to anticipate and internalize the needs, and the way ahead has to be provided to them.
- An opportunity to strengthen the capacity of developing countries to use the relevant social and economic sectors for adaptation to climate change impacts. This will help develop the capacities to identify and assess on-going and future climate risks and vulnerabilities, and to design appropriate responses to reduce vulnerability and climate change impacts.
- An opportunity to bring the major stakeholders closer around a common vision and to use needs as a national sub-national planning tool. This will enable the integration of climate policies and services at various levels, and also benefit from cross-sectoral synergies.
- A window for economic coordination among the states of the domain at the national level and disseminate best practices across regions. This will help in developing a common understanding of the challenges and opportunities that lie ahead. It will also provide an opportunity to share experiences and lessons learned, and to develop a common approach to climate change adaptation.
- An opportunity for the private sector to play a role in the implementation of climate policies and programs. This will enable the development of innovative solutions and approaches to address the challenges posed by climate change.
- An opportunity for international cooperation on climate services to be developed at national, regional and global levels. This will help to strengthen the capacity of developing countries to use the relevant social and economic sectors for adaptation to climate change impacts.

In order to do this effectively, the mandate of DMNG should be expanded to include coordination with other national institutions and organizations involved in climate change adaptation and mitigation. The DMNG should also be responsible for the development of a comprehensive strategy for the integration of climate change into national policies and programs. This will enable the DMNG to play a leading role in the development of a comprehensive strategy for the integration of climate change into national policies and programs.

## Sub-Activity 3.1: National Adaptation Planning

A clear reference point for LUP will be established as a basis for interaction between DMNG and the climate service providers. This will enable the establishment of an effective framework for the coordination of adaptation activities at the national level. The LUP is a key part of the overall strategy for the integration of climate change into national policies and programs, and it will provide a clear reference point for the development of an implementation plan for the integration of climate change into national policies and programs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

The National Climate Outlook / Monsoon Forum (NCOF) is the official component of the UIP and will be held annually to provide a platform for climate scientists to share their expertise on the seasonal outlook. The NCOF will enhance the utility of the UIP and support the implementation of the UIP.

- **Feedback** - The NCOFs will provide a platform for DNMOs to obtain feedback on how effectively they are disseminating information to end users, and to identify information needs from users. Evaluation of decision makers' information needs will be conducted through surveys and focus group discussions.
- **Dialogue** - The NCOF will build dialogue between climate services users – including government agencies, NCOFs, private sector representatives, civil society organizations, water groups and others – and the providers of climate information (such as NFCCS). The forum will promote a culture of DACCI (Demand-driven, Authoritative, Credible, Context-specific and Integrated) climate services. The NCOF will include sessions on preparedness planning, early warning systems, and other topics relevant to the new plans and decisions.
- **Outreach** - The NCOFs will capture regional common practices in the use of climate information and identify best practices that can be shared across regions. This will help to build a community of practice around climate services and promote the uptake of climate information by decision makers.
- **Evaluation** - The NCOF's role is to provide a mechanism to monitor and evaluate the development, delivery and impact of climate services. Delivery platforms such as the NFCCS provide evidence-based decision-making and risk management tools for users. The NCOFs will support the evaluation of these platforms (Sub-national Key Stakeholders (SKS) will include the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) process and provide feedback on the effectiveness of the DACCI principles in practice). The NCOF's role is to monitor the M&E process and ensure that it is effective.

This proposal is a priority area for the Global Climate Services (GCS) through the NFCCS and NCOFs. It recognizes the critical importance of ongoing dialogue as the foundation of effective climate services. The GCS will continue to engage with stakeholders in the development of regional and national climate information systems, and improve governance for the sector across the region. Monitoring dialogue through the NCOF is a key component of the GCS's work plan.

The NCOFs will also be an opportunity to establish partnerships for implementing the Early Action Protocol (EAP) and to support the UIP as outreach work on making climate information more accessible to communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL v2.1 | PAGE 8 OF 10

## Sub-Activity 1.1.3: Enhance climate data management and governance

This sub-activity will support the Government of Trinidad and Tobago in managing and using climate data and information more effectively to inform decision-making processes and improve the quality of life for its citizens. This is particularly important for the high climate-vulnerable sectors of health, agriculture, water resources, and disaster risk management. The Government of Trinidad and Tobago will be able to enhance its capacity to manage climate-related risks by improving data governance and ensuring that data is accessible at the national and international levels. This Government of Trinidad and Tobago works in close collaboration with the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and other relevant organizations.

- **Data Governance:** Through a series of consultative workshops with key stakeholders, Climate Data Strategy and Action Plan for DMAM and the Statistics Department will be developed to support improved data governance. This includes developing a national climate data management system that will enhance the use of existing statistical tools such as weather stations, remote sensing, and other environmental monitoring systems. The Government of Trinidad and Tobago will also develop a National Climate Data Management System (NCDMS) with the support of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). This system will be designed to improve data governance and ensure that climate data is accessible and used effectively.

- **Upgrading and Training:** Climate data management systems will be upgraded to enhance their capacity to manage climate-related risks. This includes upgrading existing software, hardware, and other equipment to support the use of climate data for decision-making purposes. The Government of Trinidad and Tobago will also provide training on how to use these systems effectively.

## Sub-Activity 1.1.4: Mainstream climate into knowledge in health, agriculture, disaster risk reduction and other sectors

This sub-activity aims to enhance the capacity of key stakeholders to integrate climate change considerations into their decision-making processes. This includes providing support for the development of climate-smart practices and technologies that can be used to improve the resilience of key sectors such as health, agriculture, water resources, and disaster risk reduction.

## Sub-Activity 1.1.5: Establish a framework and business model for sustainable climate services

One fundamental challenge facing the climate services sector is building the Project's work to support the development of a sustainable business model for climate services. This includes developing a framework that supports the provision of climate services in a way that is cost-effective, efficient, and effective. The Government of Trinidad and Tobago will also provide training on how to use these services effectively.

### Result 2: Strengthened observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of domain and its impacts

We will strengthen the school network and monitor Time-Lapse’s natural geological network (DNMG), enabling it to collect highly quality data at higher resolution and from a wider geographical range. This will allow us to improve our understanding of the impact of climate change on water resources, agriculture and ecosystems. We will also use this data to enhance our ability to understand the coverage of weather, climate and ocean observations. Using time-lapse technology we will be able to monitor changes in the landscape and vegetation over time.

One of the key activities is to provide Climate Change is a complex issue that requires a multi-disciplinary approach to understanding the impacts of climate change on society. We will work with partners to develop a comprehensive set of indicators that can be used to assess the impact of climate change on different sectors, including agriculture, water resources and health. We will also work with partners to develop a framework for monitoring and evaluating the effectiveness of our activities.

### Activity 2.1: Climate infrastructure and technical support to observations and monitoring

Sub-Activity 2.1.1: Capacity and expertise in monitoring climate change is limited and often not available at the national level. We will provide technical assistance and capacity building to strengthen the capacity of national meteorological and hydrological services (NMHS) and other relevant institutions to monitor climate change impacts.

The adaptation we’ll give to Time-Lapse’s hydrometeorological observation network, as described in the following sub-activities.

Weather Observing Systems (WOS) and by building capacity ensure sustainable operations and maintenance.

- The following interventions we contemplate to Time-Line's achievement of WMO Category 2 (basic) status for climate services:
  - Enhance the network of surface-based observation stations mainly through provision of additional equipment to existing stations. This will be achieved through collaboration with Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) technical assistance and capacity building. The GBON will support all basic reporting of data in compliance with Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) technical specifications, new automatic weather station (AWS), including, amongst others, automatic rain gauge, solar radiation sensor, air temperature and humidity sensors, anemometer, barometer, wind vane, etc. Based on the systematic Observations Financing Facility (OOF) x 200 k to establish the number of observation stations required for climate services. Time-Line will be responsible for the installation and maintenance of these stations. The estimated cost is USD 475k. This output enhances the requirement for WMO Category 2 status.
  - Increase el Niño/La Niña monitoring capability through enhanced data collection, analysis and dissemination. This includes the introduction of new observation stations and improved data management systems to enhance the accuracy of climate predictions.
  - Introduce weather radar for severe weather and climate monitoring through installation of a network of weather radars. This will be supported by the provision of training on the use of the equipment and build capacity for better understanding of the data applications.
  - Improve observations through compliance with WMO Integrated Global Observing System (IGOS) standards, including the implementation of quality control and calibration requirements and routine testing in accordance with WMO standards. This will be supported by the provision of training on the use of equipment and build capacity for better understanding of the data applications.
  - Build capacity to use Information and Communication Technology (ICT) in the collection and exchange of hydrometeorological observations, required for analysis, monitoring and forecasting and the dissemination of information to users. This will be supported by the provision of training on the use of ICT equipment and build capacity for better understanding of the data applications.

The Green Climate Fund Climate Finance Readiness Programme is a joint initiative between the World Bank Group and the WMO. The programme aims to support countries in their efforts to access climate finance from international sources, including the GCF. The programme provides technical assistance to countries to strengthen their national systems for climate change planning, policy and implementation.

- Establish a Quality Management System (QMS) - to be defined under Sub-Activity 2.1.2

#### Establishing the QMS
The standard established QMS will follow ISO 9001:2015 and will include the following elements:
- **Temperature, humidity, pressure** - all sensors are calibrated to NMI standards.
- **Wind speed and direction (WND)** - standards to be confirmed to OCM.
- **Precipitation** - standards to be confirmed to WMO.
- **All activities** - Temperature, Humidity, Pressure, Wind Speed and Direction (THP-WND) standards to be confirmed to the Global Climate Observing System (GCOS), which reports to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC).

The project will also support the development of a national IoT network that will be used for the collection, storage, and transmission of weather data.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.4 - Establish a National Forecasting Centre

The Project will establish a National Forecasting Centre with expertise in numerical weather prediction (NWP) and climate services. The Centre will provide short-range forecasts to support decision-making for agriculture, water resources management, energy, transport, health, and other sectors. The Centre will also provide long-range forecasts and seasonal climate predictions.

The Project will strengthen the capacity of the National Meteorological Agency to develop and implement a national climate services strategy. This will include the development of a national climate services strategy, the establishment of a national climate services centre, and the provision of training and technical assistance to support the implementation of the strategy.

A simple prediction algorithm offers the potential to provide technical support and improve capacity building for developing countries in their efforts to implement Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMAs), under the guidance of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The system can be just as useful for developed countries, where it could help to identify opportunities for reducing emissions. The system would be based on the Dynamic Climate Change and Pollution Abatement Model (DCCPAAM) developed by DMI. The system would be designed to provide a range of outputs including:

- A summary of the current state of the climate change and pollution abatement system.
- An assessment of the potential for future emissions reductions.
- A forecast of the impact of different policies on emissions, air quality, and health.

### Subsection 2.2.3 - Climate change impacts and production

The Government of Timor-Leste has acknowledged that academic institutions such as the National University of Timor-Leste (UNTL) have a key role to play in supporting the DMI. Specifically, the Centre for Climate Change and Resilience (CCCR) at UNTL is already providing support to the government in developing their NAMAs. The CCCR is also working with the DMI to develop a system that can be used to identify opportunities for reducing emissions.

### Subsection 2.3 - Climate change models

The CCCR aims to develop climate change models such as CMIP5 (Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5) and provide guidance on risk modeling and future interpretation. The DMI will work with the CCCR to develop a system that can be used to identify opportunities for reducing emissions. The DMI will also work with the CCCR to develop a system that can be used to identify opportunities for reducing emissions.

### Subsection 2.3.1 - Existing climate change models

The Project will support Timor-Leste to establish a multi-hazard impact-based forecasting approach but will not provide direct support and capacity building for DMI to perform sub-national forecasts (zones 1-3 with respect to the DMI’s existing system). Concurrently, it will support DMI’s use of key stakeholders in baseline hydrometeorological data collection, climate change impacts assessment, and development of specific decision-support systems (DSS) for agriculture, disaster risk reduction (DRR), and marine sector.

ferring communities and to enhance health for early warning and triggering of emergency ready activities to be implemented through Chapter 4.2.

- **Activity 3.2.1: Multi-Hazard Early Warning System (MHEWS)** - This intervention will work with DMNG and the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) to customize the System for Multi-Hazard Impact Based Early Warning (SMHI-BEW) that has been developed by NDMA. The system will be customized to provide early warning and advisory platform that integrates a four-tier early warning dissemination system, including community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM), local government, district level, and national level. We will also facilitate the integration of SMHI-BEW with the existing Early Warning System (EWS) in DMNG, MAFF, DID and RIMES/NARSS. The system will be integrated into the existing EWS infrastructure to provide early warning information to the communities through various channels including mobile phones, radio, television, and other media.

- **Activity 3.2.2: Multi-Hazard Impact Assessment** - This intervention will work with DMNG and NDMA to develop a Multi-Hazard Impact Assessment (MHIA) system that integrates the impact assessment of climate change on health and water resources. The MHIA system will be developed in collaboration with the Department of Meteorology, the Department of Environment, the Department of Health, and other relevant agencies. The development of the MHIA system will include the integration of climate change information into the existing early warning systems and the development of a web-based platform that can be used to assess the impact of climate change on health and water resources.

- **Activity 3.2.3: Sub-National Climate and Health Working Group** - This intervention aims to enhance coordination and collaboration between the climate and health communities in the sub-national level. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies at the sub-national level to establish a national Climate and Health Working Group (CHWG). The CHWG will provide a platform for sharing information, knowledge, and best practices on climate change and health. The CHWG will also facilitate the development of a national climate change and health strategy that can be used to guide the implementation of climate change and health interventions at the sub-national level.

- **Activity 3.2.4: Climate Change and Health Training** - This intervention aims to enhance the capacity of health workers and other relevant stakeholders to understand and respond to the impacts of climate change on health. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies to develop and deliver training programs on climate change and health. The training programs will be designed to build the knowledge and skills of health workers and other relevant stakeholders to respond to the impacts of climate change on health.

- **Activity 3.2.5: Climate Change and Health Advocacy** - This intervention aims to enhance advocacy and communication on climate change and health. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies to develop and implement a national climate change and health advocacy strategy. The advocacy strategy will be designed to raise awareness of the impacts of climate change on health and to promote action to address these impacts.

- **Activity 3.2.6: Climate Change and Health Research** - This intervention aims to enhance research on the impacts of climate change on health. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies to develop and implement a national climate change and health research agenda. The research agenda will be designed to build the evidence base for climate change and health interventions and to inform policy and practice.

- **Activity 3.2.7: Climate Change and Health Policy** - This intervention aims to enhance policy development on climate change and health. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies to develop and implement a national climate change and health policy framework. The policy framework will be designed to guide the implementation of climate change and health interventions at the sub-national level.

- **Activity 3.2.8: Climate Change and Health Monitoring and Evaluation** - This intervention aims to enhance monitoring and evaluation of climate change and health interventions. The intervention will work with NDMA, public health and environmental agencies to develop and implement a national climate change and health monitoring and evaluation system. The monitoring and evaluation system will be designed to track the progress and impact of climate change and health interventions.

## Sub-Activity 2.3.1: Establish an integrated monitoring system
This sub-activity will establish a nationally led, demand-driven, transparent, and reliable health monitoring system to assess and reduce the impacts of poor air quality on human and environmental health in five cities. Through monitoring, forecasting, real-time warning and reporting systems, the health impacts of climate change can be reduced both presently and in the future. The integrated monitoring system will include the following components:
- Air Quality Monitoring (AQM)
- Particulate Matter (PM2.5) and ground-level ozone (O3)10
- PM2.5 is a significant contributor to health including asthma, bronchitis, and other respiratory diseases.
- Nitrogen Dioxide (NO2) is a precursor for PM2.5 and contributes to cardiovascular disease.

The Project will establish a hybrid system of quality monitoring systems ensuring the best output parameters for five cities. The integrated monitoring system will include:
- MAIAC/Common Operational Data Handlers (CODH) PM2.5 satellite data
- High-resolution remote sensing data can be obtained from the European Space Agency (ESA)
- Ground-based air pollution monitoring stations and data collection
- High-resolution spatial-temporal air pollution data

The Project will provide a reliable baseline for future research on health impacts of air pollution in five cities. Establishment of the monitoring system will be critical to developing an integrated monitoring framework that can be used by other countries and regions. The Project will also develop a county-level health impact assessment (HIA) toolkit and support the development of the new indicators and datasets, the Project will deliver a county-level health impact assessment (HIA) toolkit and support the development of the new indicators and datasets.

The data will be used to inform a climate change adaptation plan that can distribute hypothetical air pollution scenarios and identify potential health impacts. Local stakeholders will engage in workshops to develop the HIA toolkit and support the development of the new indicators and datasets, the Project will deliver a county-level health impact assessment (HIA) toolkit and support the development of the new indicators and datasets.

## Sub-Activity 2.3.1 - Climate-related Diseases and Disease Support for Health
This sub-activity will support the co-development of informed intervention products that will provide decision-makers with evidence-based information on climate change impacts, health risks, and adaptation measures to reduce climate-related diseases. The Project will work with WHO and the Ministry of Health to integrate weather, climate, epidemiological and other relevant data into a comprehensive health risk assessment framework. The Project will support the development of an integrated monitoring system (IMS), which will be used to develop its implementation with the National Climate Action and Comprehensive Plan for Health.

### Sub-Activity 2.3.4: Develop a model and risk health-informed forecasts and advisories

This sub-activity will develop a model application to enhance access to health-related forecasts and advisories. The model will be developed by the National Meteorological Service (NMS) in collaboration with the Ministry of Health (MoH). The model will be developed using the ODS (Sub-Activity 2.3.3) by delivering actionable information, services, and health advisories that are tailored to the needs of the health sector. The model will develop targeted early warnings that can alert the population to risk-informed emergencies such as extreme weather events, heatstroke, UV exposure, haze, and other air pollution. The model will be developed in collaboration with local health authorities and will be guided by the localized communication strategies (Sub-Activity 2.3.5).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 40 OF 118

The Project will work with Citi Venezuela de Inversiones (CVI), a local non-governmental organization and the Ministry of Environment to promote climate change adaptation in the municipalities of Cojedes, Barinas and Portuguesa.

At the national level, the sub-national will support the Government of Venezuela to hold the National Climate Change Conference and to develop a National Adaptation Plan. The project will also provide for a finance (climate-smart) risk insurance facility that will be managed by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources. It will also promote climate change adaptation in the municipalities of Cojedes, Barinas and Portuguesa through a community-based approach to climate change adaptation. This will include the development of a community-based early warning system for extreme weather events and the establishment of community-based disaster risk management committees (CBDM) and local government-level actors.

At the community level, the Project will use participatory approaches such as risk mapping and focus group discussions to identify climate risks and vulnerabilities. It will also support the development of community-based adaptation plans that include early warning systems for extreme weather events and the establishment of community-based disaster risk management committees (CBDM) and local government-level actors.

The sub-national will contract community-based working groups (Inclu-Less in each area) to lead the implementation of climate change adaptation activities. These groups will be trained in participatory approaches such as risk mapping, focus group discussions and community-based disaster risk management committees (CBDM). The project will also support the development of a community-based early warning system for extreme weather events.

This sub-activity will enable an integrated and holistic approach to build the technological, technical and institutional capacity of local governments to manage climate risks and disasters. The project will also support the development of a community-based early warning system for extreme weather events.

The Project will ensure that such is done in 15 Municipal Disaster Management Committees (NDRM) of these states. This sub-activity will be implemented by the NDRM with technical assistance from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MINAM). - **Stakeholder Identification**: The Proposal will identify key stakeholders to be involved in the development and implementation of the GCF, including international, national, regional and local actors and their agencies.

- **Risk Assessment**: The Assessment of risks is a participatory process to involve risk actors, key stakeholders and other relevant parties. It will include an analysis of existing risks and how they have increased or been reduced. The assessment will provide an overview of the different types of early actions that can be taken to reduce risks (e.g., climate change adaptation measures such as water, soil, etc.).

- **Impact-based Forecasting (Trigger)**: The trigger is the greatest threshold value based on weather and climate indicators that will activate the forecast-based financing activity. The trigger model is to be developed based on existing analysis, and alongside of forecasting activities.

- **Financial Mechanism**: The Proposal will describe a custom-tailored, scalable financial mechanism for GCF that provides a sustainable source of funding for early actions.

  The first phase in development of the Proposal will comprise a scoping study that will cover feasible options to develop a financial mechanism. This may be embedded in existing initiatives, or we have the need and resources to develop our own (e.g., a trust fund, an insurance scheme) and/or in a dialogue with them and the potential for GCF’s effectiveness and added value to enhance the overall impact.

  Following the scoping phase, the second phase of Proposal development will consist of collaborative consultations with institutional stakeholders and local communities to define free key elements that could be included in the financial mechanism.

- **Defined triggers, based on improved forecasts, for pre-arranged actions among a working losses and damages insurance scheme**: A trigger is defined as an event that can activate the insurance scheme. The scheme will be activated when the threshold of the possible early warning information derived from forecasts that can trigger the early actions.

  A climate change adaptation insurance scheme is currently being developed and tested in several countries, including the Philippines, Vietnam, and Colombia. A similar approach could be applied to the GCF.

- **Forecasting**: Forecasting is one of the key elements of the Proposal. Society supports V2.1 Assessments; the Proposal will undertake a feasibility study on the CYTLab’s first regional forecasting and early warning system for climate-related risks. This will include developing a prototype that can be used to provide early warnings and risk information to communities, governments, and other stakeholders. The Proposal will also explore how to integrate this into the GCF.

UNEP will be the Accredited Entity for the Project and will be responsible for managing the implementation, monitoring and reporting of the project activities. UNEP will also provide technical assistance to the government in the preparation of the Project Proposal and ensure that it is aligned with the Funded Activity Agreement to be signed between GCF and UNDP and the Accreditation Board.

Through its Science Division, UNEP has been funding expertise in environmental and climate change information management and early warning systems. For example, with GEO and EC funding it is currently developing new tools for the integration of environmental data into decision support systems for reporting on SDGs and MEAs. In its Science Division, UNEP manages the CLIVARIN and County Climate Change Network (CCCN) projects that provide climate information to national meteorological services and other users. Through these networks, UNEP also convenes and facilitates regional environmental information networks and the public dissemination of data and information on climate change. UNEP's work in this area is closely linked with its work on environmental knowledge management and capacity building.

UNEP is also a key player in the "One Health" approach - a cross-cutting and systems approach to health that recognizes the interconnections between people, animals, plants and their shared environment. The One Health approach aims to improve human, animal and ecosystem health by addressing the root causes of illness, injury and disease. This includes the development of integrated solutions on food, water, land and environmental health, as well as the promotion of healthy ecosystems. In addition, the United Nations County (UNCC) works to ensure that regional and national governments are able to access and use climate information for decision-making.

# Technical Partners

The project will benefit from the expertise of a broad coalition of Technical Partners who have long-standing experience and proven track records in climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and related fields. These include FAO, RIMES, IFC, (including CIMMYT), CIAT, World Bank, BMGF, and TCP. The Technical Partners will be engaged to provide technical assistance, capacity development, and knowledge management for the project. They will also support the development of partnerships in the Asia-Pacific region. Based on the priorities of Time-Line, the Technical Partners will help or co-lead specific activities under the project. These include:

- Provision of technical expertise
- Development of guidelines and tools
- Support to capacity building initiatives

The partners will be engaged through a combination of short-term and long-term assignments.

## Agency supporting national implementation

### Details of technical partner and track record

#### Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO)

FAO is an experienced agency of the United Nations with a proven track record in climate-smart agriculture. FAO has extensive experience in:

- Climate change adaptation
- Climate change mitigation
- Climate change impact assessments
- Climate change policy development

FAO is an established GO-GA partner and has relevant expertise in managing similar projects.

#### International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

IFAD is a long-standing GO-GA partner with extensive experience in agricultural development. IFAD has:

- Supported the development of climate-smart agriculture
- Provided technical assistance to farmers
- Facilitated the adoption of climate-smart practices

IFAD has a strong track record in implementing projects that support smallholder farmers and rural communities.

### Rules to project activities

#### FAO and IFAD

FAO and IFAD will jointly deliver advisory services to national governments. They will:

- Provide technical assistance
- Develop guidelines and tools
- Facilitate the adoption of climate-smart practices

These agencies will work closely with national governments to ensure that the project meets its objectives.

## Regional engagement Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems (MHEWS)

### Details of technical partner and track record

#### World Meteorological Organization (WMO)

WMO is an experienced agency in the development and implementation of MHEWS. WMO has:

- Supported the development of climate-smart agriculture
- Provided technical assistance to farmers
- Facilitated the adoption of climate-smart practices

WMO has a strong track record in implementing projects that support smallholder farmers and rural communities.

### Rules to project activities

#### WMO

WMO will provide technical assistance to national governments. They will:

- Develop guidelines and tools
- Provide training and capacity building
- Facilitate the adoption of climate-smart practices

These agencies will work closely with national governments to ensure that the project meets its objectives.

### Project Steering Committee

The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be established comprising the NDA node serving as a national EE and UNEP. It will meet at least once per year and will be co-chaired by the NDA and the AE UNEP. The PSC will provide overall guidance to the project, ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved project document, and oversee the decision-making body within the Project governance structure, and all related review and monitoring arrangements.

The functions of the Steering Committee are:

- Ensuring full implementation of joint and inclusive engagement under the Project is maintained.
- Ensuring full coordination of various regular and national stakeholders under their jurisdictions to provide guidance on project implementation.
- Representing the interests of civil society and communities derived from a regular formal dialogue between the GGA and relevant peer bodies.
- Reviewing and monitoring progress in project implementation, and
- Providing strategic advice on the use of GCF-funded projects (in particular, the UNDP-Acc FP1126) as an effective operationalized.

### Project Implementation Committee

The Project Implementation Committee will be the NDA, EE UNEP, RE Representative and national EE representatives.

#### Functions of the Project Implementation Committee:

- The Project Manager;
- Representatives from the Technical Partner agencies involved in Project implementation - FAO, RMES, GGA, NDA;
- Representatives from additional entities involved in Project implementation, in particular GNNAD, NDAD;
- Representatives of civil society and communities organizations; and
- Representatives of the private sector.

# B. Justification for GCF funding request (page 1 of 8)

Taro-Leh is a Least Developed Country (LDC) and Small Island Developing State (SIDS) and a post-conflict country with a population of approximately 30,000 people. The country has been in a state of emergency since the early 1970s due to internal conflict. In 1995, the government signed a peace agreement with the opposition and a ceasefire was declared. In 2008, the government signed a comprehensive peace agreement.

Taro-Leh is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world. It has an area of approximately 160 square kilometers and a population of around 30,000 people. The country is highly dependent on agriculture and fishing for its livelihood. In recent years, the country has been affected by climate change, which has led to increased flooding and droughts.

The country's economy is heavily dependent on agriculture and fishing. The agricultural sector accounts for approximately 80% of the country's GDP and employs around 70% of the population. The country's main exports are fish and agricultural products. The country is also a major tourist destination, with tourism accounting for around 15% of the country's GDP.

The country has a high level of poverty and inequality. Around 40% of the population lives below the poverty line and around 30% of the population is undernourished. The country has a low level of education and health care, with only around 60% of the population having access to basic education and health care.

The country's infrastructure is limited and there are few roads and bridges. The country has no electricity grid and relies on diesel generators for power. The country also has limited access to clean water and sanitation.

The country's climate change vulnerability is exacerbated by its small size, low-lying nature, and dependence on agriculture and fishing. The country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of storms, and changes in precipitation patterns.

The country's greenhouse gas emissions are relatively low compared to other countries. However, the country's high level of poverty and inequality means that it is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The country's limited infrastructure and lack of access to clean water and sanitation also make it more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

The country has a high level of dependence on foreign aid and assistance. The country receives around $50 million in foreign aid each year, which accounts for around 30% of its GDP. The country's high level of poverty and inequality means that it is particularly dependent on foreign aid to support its development efforts.

The country's climate change vulnerability is exacerbated by its small size, low-lying nature, and dependence on agriculture and fishing. The country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of storms, and changes in precipitation patterns.

The country has a high level of poverty and inequality. Around 40% of the population lives below the poverty line and around 30% of the population is undernourished. The country has a low level of education and health care, with only around 60% of the population having access to basic education and health care.

The country's infrastructure is limited and there are few roads and bridges. The country has no electricity grid and relies on diesel generators for power. The country also has limited access to clean water and sanitation.

The country's climate change vulnerability is exacerbated by its small size, low-lying nature, and dependence on agriculture and fishing. The country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of storms, and changes in precipitation patterns.

system with the support of IMES Time-Lens's Business Development will also be supported in their climate change adaptation work. Their expertise in bringing data sharing and collaborative planning DMAG and other key stakeholders will be enhanced through the use of the Digital Management System (DMS), Disaster Management Information System (DMIS) and Disaster Risk Management Information System (DRMIS). These systems will support the development of a comprehensive risk management system that includes early warning, monitoring, assessments and enable the integration of hazard and vulnerability information.

## Result 1
Will fund the acquisition and installation of new technology and build DMAG capacity to create a robust data sharing platform. This will also include the development of a Multi-Hazard Early Warning Assessment and Emergency Response System (MHEWAS) that will be based on Disaster Management Information Systems (DMIS). The system will be developed with the support of key stakeholders and will be integrated into the existing systems to ensure a hybrid approach. The technology will also develop the capacity for the development of plans and processes, including early warning systems, risk assessments and response planning. This will also include a hybrid approach as quality monitoring system. Targeted capacity development in the DMAG, local government and communities will be supported to enhance their understanding and benefit from the new technologies in the long term. Risk management in the communities will be enhanced through the use of these systems.

## Result 2
Will build national capacity to deliver effective Early Warning Systems (EWS) and work with key stakeholders to develop a comprehensive EWS that provides timely and accurate information to the farmers they advise. We will also work with communities to develop community-based early warning systems and ensure that they are integrated into existing systems.

## Result 3
Will enhance disaster preparedness capabilities from the national to community level through the development of a comprehensive Disaster Risk Management (DRM) system. This will include the development of a National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA), which will be responsible for the overall coordination and implementation of DRM activities, agencies (Disaster Management Authorities) and districts that will implement DRM with support from the NDMA. The NDMA will also provide technical assistance to local governments in the development and implementation of their own disaster management plans and processes. This will include the development of a comprehensive early warning system for extreme climate events, including through

# D EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The performance of the proposal against the programming criteria set out in the GCF’s Investment Criteria (IC) and Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS).

## D1 Impact assessment (SSA) with approximated 1 page

### The Project

The Project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by avoiding baseline deforestation making and through improved management of existing forests. This will be achieved by implementing energy efficiency measures, promoting renewable energy, and improving forest management practices. The project will also support the development of local communities by providing training and capacity building programs.

### Benefits

- Reduces carbon emissions by 1,203,719 tonnes (51%) more than baseline.
- Improves livelihoods for 84,678 people (49%) through improved access to renewable energy.
- Enhances resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems.
- Supports the achievement of the GCF Performance Measurement Framework (PMF).

### At a Glance

- Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities, and ecosystems.
- The Project aims to improve the resilience of 1,203,719 people (51%) more than baseline (PMF target).
- This represents an increase in resilience of 49% compared to the baseline.
- The Project is expected to reduce emissions by 84,678 tonnes (49%) more than baseline.

### Financial Analysis

The estimated cost of the project is USD 10 million with a 10-year duration. The economic benefit ratio of carbon (EBR) of 1.25 exceeds the minimum EBR threshold of 1.0 set by the GCF. The total key parameters such as the discount rate, inflation, and exchange rates are considered in the analysis.

- **NPV**: USD 8 million
- **IRR**: 17%
- **EBR**: 1.25

The financial analysis indicates that the project is financially viable and provides a positive return on investment. The economic benefits of the project exceed the costs, making it a sound investment for the GCF.

### Social and Environmental Impact

The Project will have significant social and environmental impacts. It will reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 1,203,719 tonnes (51%) more than baseline and improve livelihoods for 84,678 people (49%). The project will also support the development of local communities by providing training and capacity building programs.

- **Social Impact**: Improved access to renewable energy, increased resilience, and enhanced livelihoods.
- **Environmental Impact**: Reduced greenhouse gas emissions, improved forest management practices, and enhanced biodiversity.

The Project is expected to have a positive impact on both the environment and society. It will reduce greenhouse gas emissions, improve livelihoods, and enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities.
