# Enhancing Early Warning Systems to Build Greater Resilience to Hydro-meteorological Hazards in Asia

## Annex B - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### 1. Introduction: Rationale of Gender Mainstreaming for the Project

This Gender Assessment has been prepared as an input for the design of the Great Climate Fund (GCF) project "Enhancing Early Warning Systems to Build Greater Resilience to Hydro-meteorological Hazards in Asia." The project includes a specific gender component. The project will be implemented by UNDP and executed by the Government of Bangladesh, with technical lead from the Government of Japan.

The project aims to enhance the development and effective use of hydro-meteorological services for disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. It will support the implementation of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015-2030) and contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 1, SDG 5, and SDG 13.

The project will focus on the following outcomes:

- Improved access to climate information services.
- Enhanced capacity for disaster risk management.
- Strengthened institutional arrangements for climate information services.
- Increased use of climate information services by users in vulnerable sectors.
- Enhanced gender equality and social inclusion in climate information services.

### 2. Gender Assessment Report Summary

The Gender Assessment Report highlights that women are not chemically vulnerable to climate change because they have biological sex and the bodies on which they exist. Women are more likely to be affected by climate change, including extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and storms. Women are also more likely to be affected by climate change-related health issues.

The report finds that women are more likely to be affected by climate change because they have biological sex and the bodies on which they exist. Women are more likely to be affected by climate change, including extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and storms. Women are also more likely to be affected by climate change-related health issues.

The report finds that women are more likely to be affected by climate change because they have biological sex and the bodies on which they exist. Women are more likely to be affected by climate change, including extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and storms. Women are also more likely to be affected by climate change-related health issues.

### 3. Gender Equality Action Plan

The Gender Equality Action Plan is a cross-cutting issue that will be addressed throughout the project cycle. The action plan includes the following activities:

- Conduct gender analysis.
- Develop gender-responsive policies and procedures.
- Provide training on gender equality and social inclusion.
- Establish gender equality monitoring and evaluation mechanisms.
- Ensure equal participation of women in decision-making processes.

The action plan will be implemented by UNDP, with technical lead from the Government of Japan. The project will be executed by the Government of Bangladesh, with support from the Great Climate Fund (GCF).

### 4. References

- Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (2015-2030)
- Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)
- Great Climate Fund (GCF) Guidelines
- UNDP Gender Equality Policy

# Interventions for Producing Tobacco-Optimal Customers

Interventions that produce tobacco-optimal customers, or even enforce the challenges faced by stakeholders in the tobacco industry, are essential to understand. These interventions include the promotion of health, education and behavior modification, codes to produce safer, healthier and more environmentally friendly products, and the elimination of tobacco-related diseases. The tobacco industry is a complex system that includes multiple stakeholders such as farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. Furthermore, gender issues all need to be considered in order to achieve the objectives and deliver fundamental impacts across Time-Level 1.

## Methodology

This study was conducted by the authors' research team, which included graduate students, postgraduate students, and other researchers. The research team used a mixed-methods approach that combined qualitative and quantitative methods. The qualitative data were collected through interviews with key informants, while the quantitative data were collected through surveys.

### Primary Data Collection

Primary data collection included an inventory of tobacco-related literature, interviews, and secondary sources. Further details are provided in Appendix 1.

### Literature Review

The literature review was conducted on global change adaptation, climate information and early warning systems, and gender issues related to the tobacco industry. The literature review focused on relevant reports from international organizations such as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), and the World Health Organization (WHO). The literature review was conducted using a systematic approach that included a search of relevant databases, journals, and other sources. The literature review was conducted to identify gaps in the existing knowledge about gender issues related to tobacco production.

### Stakeholder Consultations

Stakeholder consultations were held with key informants from the tobacco industry, including farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. The consultations were conducted using a semi-structured approach that included open-ended questions and follow-up questions. The consultations were conducted to gather information about gender issues related to tobacco production.

### Beliefs and Attitudes

Beliefs and attitudes related to gender issues in the tobacco industry were identified through interviews with key informants from the tobacco industry, including farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. The beliefs and attitudes were identified using a semi-structured approach that included open-ended questions and follow-up questions. The beliefs and attitudes were identified to understand the barriers and challenges faced by women in the tobacco industry.

### Baseline and Gender Baseline for Time-Level 1

Baseline data on gender issues related to tobacco production were collected through interviews with key informants from the tobacco industry, including farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. The baseline data were collected using a semi-structured approach that included open-ended questions and follow-up questions. The baseline data were collected to establish a baseline for the impact of interventions aimed at addressing gender issues related to tobacco production.

### Baseline and Gender Baseline for Time-Level 2

Baseline data on gender issues related to tobacco production were collected through interviews with key informants from the tobacco industry, including farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. The baseline data were collected using a semi-structured approach that included open-ended questions and follow-up questions. The baseline data were collected to establish a baseline for the impact of interventions aimed at addressing gender issues related to tobacco production.

### Baseline and Gender Baseline for Time-Level 3

Baseline data on gender issues related to tobacco production were collected through interviews with key informants from the tobacco industry, including farmers, tobacco companies, and consumers. ## Complete indices

### Human Development Index (HDI)

The HDI is a statistical tool used to measure a country's overall advancement in its population's health, knowledge, and standard of living. It combines indicators of life expectancy, education, and per capita income into a single number. The average HDI for the East Asia and Pacific region was 74.1 and for Sub-Saharan Africa it was 569.72).

The inequality-adjusted Human Development Index (IHDI) adjusts the HDI to account for inequalities in opportunities for human development. It is calculated as a percentage of the maximum possible value of the HDI. The average loss due to inequality in the region was 18%.

### Gender Development Index (GDI)

The GDI is defined as a ratio of the female to male HDI. The GDI measures gender inequalities in human development by comparing female and male HDIs for each country. It is calculated using the same formula as the HDI, but with separate values for females and males.

### Gender Inequality Index (GII)

The GII is a comprehensive measure of inequality between men and women. It is measured as the loss in human development due to gender inequality. The GII covers three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and labor market participation. Lack of access to sexual and reproductive health care, unequal opportunities for women in education and employment, and unequal participation in political life are some of the factors that contribute to the GII.

### Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI)

The MPI identifies people living in poverty based on their health, education, and standard of living. Population groups are classified according to their level of deprivation in these three dimensions: health, education, and standard of living. The MPI is calculated using the same methodology as the global MPI. In 2018, 39% of the population lived below the poverty line.

The report on the development of the region highlights the progress made by countries in reducing poverty and improving human development. However, it also notes that there are still significant challenges to be addressed in order to achieve sustainable development goals.

# Education

Traditional norms and practices (e.g., Tuberculosis). The disease has continued spreading despite efforts to prevent it. In some areas, girls and boys are both at primary school age; in others, only boys are at this level of education. In some cases, girls are not allowed to attend school because they have reached puberty.

Limiting the spread of tuberculosis is a complex challenge presented by poverty, social exclusion, and inadequate health care systems. These factors have been noted in various parts of the world. For example, in 2010, only 38% of women had received at least some primary education compared to 54% of men. In sub-Saharan Africa, the proportion with a primary education is low: about 69% for men and 47% for women aged 25 years and above compared with 81% for men and 63% for women in high-income countries.

Among adults, women are much more likely to have never attended school. Half of women aged 25 years and older had no education in 2010; this proportion was higher among rural residents (74%) than urban ones (58%). Women who lack education are less likely to be aware of tuberculosis and its treatment, and they may not seek care when ill. Gender disparities in education and health levels result in differing capacities of women and men to access and use health services.

Life expectancy at birth is a major indicator of a population's health. In Tuberculosis, life expectancy at birth was 67 years for women and 71 years for men in 2010; this compares with an average of 83 years for women and 79 years for men globally.

# (89 per 100,000 live births.)[^1] Continued effort will be required to reduce its MMR to less than 70 by 2030.

The global mortality rate has increased from 65 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2009 to 80 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2019. This increase is attributed to a variety of factors including the impact of climate change and conflict on maternal health. The World Health Organization (WHO) has identified several key interventions that can reduce maternal mortality, such as access to quality care during pregnancy and childbirth.

Timor-Leste is one of the highest-risk countries in the world, with 48% of children born at home without skilled birth attendance. In 2019, the maternal mortality ratio (MMR) was estimated to be between 367 and 580 deaths per 100,000 live births, compared with rates of 189, 24% and 43%, respectively.[^2] The neonatal mortality rate is also high at 37 deaths per 1,000 live births. The infant mortality rate is estimated to be between 56 and 93 deaths per 1,000 live births.

The Global Hunger Index (GHI) is a composite measure of undernourishment, child mortality, and low birth weight. Timor-Leste's GHI score in 2019 was 47.8, indicating a serious level of hunger. The country ranks 103rd out of 117 countries, with the majority having less than 5% of their population experiencing hunger.

## 3.5 Work and economic empowerment

In Timor-Leste, there is a high gender disparity in employment and labor force participation. Women make up only 27% of the formal workforce compared to 48% of men. This disparity is even more pronounced among those employed in the informal sector, where women account for just 15% of the workforce.

The majority of women (63%) are engaged in agriculture and fishing, while men are more likely to be involved in construction and manufacturing. Women also tend to work longer hours than men, often without pay or outside of a labor force structure. This gender disparity is exacerbated by cultural norms that limit women's participation in the workforce.

The reported employment rate is higher for women (48%) compared to men (31%), but this figure includes both formal and informal employment. The majority of employed women work in agriculture, while men are more likely to be engaged in construction and manufacturing.

Men are the main income providers in Timor-Leste, with only 24% of household income assigned to women. This gender disparity is reflected in the lower educational attainment of women compared to men.

[^2]: Source: United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF), State of the World's Children Report 2019.

working for a family farmer or agricultural artisan[^1]. Reliance on a man's income alone is a significant barrier to women's economic empowerment and their ability to invest in income-generating activities.

Urbanization is a major means to overcome the poor living conditions that rural women face. It is known that urbanization is linked to higher levels of education, better access to health services, and improved livelihoods. In Thailand, Landesa-Titli has seen more women than men moving to urban areas in search of better opportunities.

Promoting the economic empowerment of women is essential for the development of women's rights, as well as the achievement of gender equality goals with an economic growth, women's inclusion in decision-making processes and their participation in the labor market.

### 3.6 Government subsidence and action making

In Thailand, traditional social and cultural norms affect and gender roles for men and women. These norms are deeply rooted in the patriarchal system that has existed for centuries. Women have been largely excluded from land ownership and decision-making processes. In 2018, only 7% of landowners were women. However, in July 2016, a new law was passed to allow women to inherit land from their fathers. This law has increased the number of women who own land. In addition, the number of women Village Chiefs elected doubled, with more than 50% of the total.

Despite this progress, significant cultural barriers still prevent women making decisions within their communities and participating in local governance. Land ownership is a key factor in determining a woman's status in society. Women are often excluded from land ownership due to cultural norms that prevent them from inheriting or owning land.

At the national level, Landesa-Titli has made significant progress towards achieving gender equality in land rights. In 2018, women accounted for more than 45% of the total number of people who received land titles. This progress has been aided by the government's commitment to promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. The government has also taken steps to address the cultural barriers that prevent women from owning land.

### 3.7 Land and assets ownership

Land is a critical resource for agricultural production in Thailand, given that over 80% of the land is used for agriculture. Women are more likely than men to be involved in farming activities and have a greater need for access to land. However, women are often excluded from land ownership due to cultural norms that prevent them from inheriting or owning land.

Women's participation in agricultural production is essential for economic development and food security. Women are also more likely to be involved in the management of household resources, including land. Therefore, it is important to promote women's access to land and other assets.

# Gender-based violence

## Violence against women and girls in Timor-Leste is widespread and cuts across the country’s income levels.

Violence against women and girls in Timor-Leste is widespread and cuts across the country's income levels. The United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) reports that 59% of women aged 15-49 have experienced physical or sexual violence from an intimate partner, while 62% have experienced emotional abuse. The UNFPA also notes that 36% of women have experienced sexual violence and 86% have experienced physical violence.

## Gender-based violence in Timor-Leste

Timor-Leste signed the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 2015, which defined and condemned all forms of discrimination against women. The government has also implemented laws that protect women from domestic violence and sexual harassment. However, despite these efforts, gender-based violence remains a significant issue in Timor-Leste.

### Gender-Based Violence: Legal and Institutional Framework in Timor-Leste

The Government of Timor-Leste is committed to the promotion of gender equality under its Constitution, which prohibits discrimination based on sex. The government has also ratified several international conventions to promote women's rights, including:

- Gender equality as a cross-cutting priority is also reflected in the Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan 2011-2030.
- Gender mainstreaming systems and mechanisms.
- Gender responsive policies and laws at national and local levels.
- Employment of women through institutional support and education programs.
- Increased appointment of women in the civil service to ensure gender balance in decision-making.

# SEEP was established in 2008 as a legal agency and center governed by the advice of the SEE coordinator with Gender Working Groups (GWGs) to ensure that gender priorities are considered in all activities.

The SEE coordinator with Gender Working Groups (GWGs) is sure that gender priorities are considered in all activities. The GWGs also coordinate and monitor the implementation of the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP) and ensure that gender issues are included in national and regional strategies, policies, and programs.

The 2014 UNDP Country Report on the Lao PDR reported that SEEM and the GWGs have been able to provide technical support for gender mainstreaming. With the support of the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP), the Gender Working Groups (GWGs) are able to provide technical support for gender mainstreaming.

Lack of technical capacity to gender mainstream, especially in the use of gender analysis tools and indicators, has limited the ability of government agencies to support adaptation and disaster risk management initiatives. The lack of technical capacity to gender mainstream has also limited the ability of government agencies to support adaptation and disaster risk management initiatives.

In 2014, the Lao PDR adopted the Decentralization Decree Law (Decree No. 42014), which has strengthened the role of local governments in planning and implementing local development activities. As such, government authorities at municipal and provincial levels will be instrumental in ensuring that gender equality is integrated into local development plans.

## 4.3 Civil society organizations

### Association Legale la Piste no Lao (ALPL)

The ALPL supports a fair and just justice system by providing legal assistance to the poor and vulnerable groups, including women and children. The ALPL also provides legal aid to victims of human trafficking and sexual exploitation. The ALPL is a member of the Lao Bar Association.

### Foundation Alisa (Alisa Foundation)

The Alisa Foundation is a non-profit organization that works with the government to promote gender equality in Laos. The foundation provides technical assistance to the government on issues related to gender equality, including the promotion of women's rights and the prevention of violence against women. The foundation also provides training and education to women and girls on their rights and how to advocate for them.

### Geneva Community Centre

The Geneva Community Centre is a non-profit organization that provides legal aid and support to the poor and vulnerable in Laos. The centre provides free legal advice and representation to those who cannot afford it. The centre also provides training and education to lawyers and law students on human rights and the rule of law. The centre works closely with the government and other organizations to promote access to justice for all.

# Covalene Community Center (CCC)

Covalene Community Center (CCC) supports women's groups with a focus on strengthening their capacity to address issues of gender inequality and empowering them to become agents of change in their communities.

## Projects

### FOODSPHERE

FOODSPHERE was established in 1987 to address gender-based violence and human rights violations. It has been instrumental in providing services for women and children who are victims of abuse, including:

- **Counseling:** Provides counseling support through the involvement of 31 community-based women's groups in eight provinces.
- **Economic Empowerment:** Assists in economic empowerment, employment, and community awareness building.

### Reproductive Health

Reproductive Health is a branch of sexual education. In Tondo-Legaspi, it aims to promote gender health and women's empowerment through:

- **Healthy Living:** Encourages healthy living and family planning.
- **Counseling:** Provides counseling services for women with reproductive health issues.
- **Education:** Offers educational programs on reproductive health.

### 5.1 Agriculture and Food Security

Agriculture is the main economic activity in Tondo-Legaspi. Women play an important role as farmers, engaged in various agricultural activities such as:

- **Farming:** Engaged with 95% of men; "Dahit" the dominant form of agriculture.
- **Crop Production:** Focuses on crop production and livestock farming.
- **Livelihood Support:** Provides livelihood support to women's groups.

### 5.2 Environmental Health

The National Disaster Risk Management Directorate (NDRMD) is responsible for providing disaster risk management coordination and technical support to Tondo-Legaspi. It aims at the country's capacity for disaster risk reduction and management, including:

- **Community-Based Disaster Risk Management:** Focuses on community-based disaster risk management.
- **Environmental Health:** Promotes environmental health and safety.

### 5.3 Gender and Development

The Gender and Development (GAD) program is designed to promote gender equality and empower women in Tondo-Legaspi. It aims at:

- **Gender Equality:** Promotes gender equality and women's empowerment.
- **Community Empowerment:** Encourages community-based initiatives for gender equality.

the population will not have access to a centralized grid in the next 20 years, due to them being located in remote areas. This is due to the high cost of infrastructure and the lack of economic growth. Affordable renewable energy solutions are needed to ensure that these communities can develop and live sustainably.

Wood-fuel is mostly used by households heading a cooking purpose. It is estimated to account for 60% of the total energy consumption in Africa. The average household uses about 3.7 tons per year, which is more than double the global average. This high level of wood fuel use has significant environmental and health impacts due to the amount of time spent collecting firewood, the impact on the environment, and the health implications for women and children who are most often responsible for collecting firewood. The lack of access to clean cooking solutions also contributes to indoor air pollution, which is a major cause of death in Africa.

List of issues to directly affect a reliance on fuel wood has significant impacts to the affected communities in Time-Late, including:

- Increased time spent by women and children looking for fuel.
- Reduced time available for activities such as schoolwork, earning an income, and personal leisure.
- Increased risk of respiratory diseases and general ill health due to exposure to wood smoke.
- High levels of indoor air pollution from cooking with wood, which is a major cause of death in Africa.
- Access to sustainable energy and infrastructure is limited.

### 5.4 Health

#### 5.4.1 Water

Water-related disease (WASH) is a national problem in Time-Late. A lack of access to clean water and sanitation facilities has led to an increase in waterborne diseases. Households have limited or no access to proper sanitation facilities, and over 40% rely on unimproved water sources.

Gender is a major factor in WASH. It is predominantly women who collect and use water and provide food for their families. Women spend more time collecting water than men do, and this can lead to health problems such as back pain and injuries from carrying heavy loads of water. Women also bear the brunt of caring for sick family members due to waterborne diseases.

The lack of access to clean water and sanitation facilities has a significant impact on the health of women and children in Time-Late. This is due to the increased risk of waterborne diseases, which can lead to death or long-term health problems. The lack of access to proper sanitation facilities also leads to an increase in the spread of disease.

#### 5.4.2 Rural Health Facilities

Rural health facilities (RHUs) provide essential services to the rural population in Time-Late. However, these facilities are often understaffed and lack the necessary resources to provide adequate care. This is due to a number of factors, including:

- A lack of funding for RHUs.
- A shortage of qualified staff.
- A lack of equipment and supplies.

These factors contribute to the poor quality of care provided by RHUs, which can lead to an increase in morbidity and mortality rates among the rural population. The lack of access to proper healthcare facilities also leads to an increase in the spread of disease.

# Gender and Climate Change in Three Lessons

## Lesson 1: Gender Issues Related to Climate Change Impacts and Risks

Climate change and related disasters have differential impacts on women and men because of their provision of care work, gender roles, and responsibilities within households. Women are more vulnerable to climate-related impacts. In a vulnerability assessment conducted by WHO in November 2017, the impacts of climate change on health were assessed.

### Flood

Loss of lives and properties are key concerns expressed by workshop participants. WHO has identified that women and children are most vulnerable during floods. The elderly and people with disabilities are also at risk. Women are not seen mostly because they are involved in rescue operations. Communities may be able to reduce the impact of flooding through early warning systems, community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM), and preparedness planning.

### Drought

Tragic loss frequently suffers from the impacts of extreme and extended drought. In 2015, the International Partnership for Humanitarian Assistance (IPHA) assessed the 2017 drought fund but found that it was not enough to meet the needs of the affected population. The humanitarian response plan has been set at $77 million. This publication in this issue is a call to action for governments and international organizations to be more active for women and children who are most vulnerable.

### Landslides

Landslides can result in death and injury. Each image recorded in Three Lessons is a natural hazard that was assessed conducted by IRI in 2015. Surprisingly, it indicated that the elderly and people with disabilities were at risk during landslides. The elderly and people with disabilities are also at risk of being injured or killed.

## Lesson 2: Gender Issues in Accessing Climate Information and Early Warning Services

The key gender issues raised by the analysis in relation to access to climate information and early warning services include:

- **Accessibility**: Women may have limited access to climate information due to cultural norms and lack of education.
- **Relevance**: Climate information must be relevant to women's needs, such as agricultural practices and health risks.
- **Timeliness**: Information should be provided in a timely manner to allow for effective response.

Women's participation in decision-making processes is crucial for ensuring that climate services meet their needs. This includes involving women in the design, implementation, and evaluation of early warning systems.

*Source: Humanitarian Partnership Agreement*

**Page 161**

- Social economics, culture, politics and institutional factors contribute to men and women having different access to information and communication technologies (ICTs).

- General services are publicly unavailable in Timor-Leste and there is no systematic early warning system for humanitarian crises. Telephone lines are used by men to communicate with family members living abroad. Women use mobile phones to communicate with their husbands who work overseas.

- Disasters can exacerbate social norms that limit women's control and rights to and ownership of assets and resources, and prevent them from accessing information and communication technologies (ICTs).

- Access to reliable information and early warnings within gender groups offers different benefits to men and women. Early warning systems for rural communities are often based on local knowledge and rely on community radio stations as the main source of information.

- Women and men have different information requirements due to gender-based divisions of labour, and their roles in the household. Women are more concerned about the safety of their children during disasters. Men are more concerned with property and assets of livelihoods.

- Women and men have different capacities to understand and use disaster and early warning information.

- Women's lack of access to credit limits their ability to invest in unseasonal crops and they rely on family members for financial support. Lack of education also limits women's capacity to understand the technical language used by meteorologists, and their ability to interpret weather forecasts.

### 7. Recommendations for a Gender Transformative Approach

- Community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) should be integrated into national policies and planning frameworks.

- Gender equality should be mainstreamed in all aspects of CBDRM programming.

- Women's participation in decision-making processes at the local level is essential to ensure that their needs are met.

- Women's groups should be supported to participate in community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM) activities.

- Women and men should have equal access to information, communication technologies and early warning systems.

- Women and men should have equal access to credit and financial services.

- Women and men should have equal access to education and training opportunities.

- Women and men should have equal access to health care and social protection services.

strategically providing an entry point or opportunity to advocate or demonstrate gender equity in health and development programming. The World Health Organization (WHO) and the Global Health Workforce Alliance are committed to ensuring that the Gender-Sensitive Programming Tool is integrated into existing programs and policies, and that it is used to inform future program design.

Gender-sensitive programming refers to programs where gender norms, roles, and relationships have been considered and addressed in order to ensure that both women and men benefit equally from health interventions. Such programs go beyond treating gender as a variable and instead address the underlying causes of gender inequality and aim to transform gender relations.

Gender-responsive programming refers to programs where gender norms, roles, and relationships are actively challenged and transformed through program design and implementation. Such programs go beyond treating gender as a variable and instead address the underlying causes of gender inequality and aim to transform gender relations.

Gender-transformative programming refers to programs where gender norms, roles, and relationships are actively challenged and transformed through program design and implementation. Such programs go beyond treating gender as a variable and instead address the underlying causes of gender inequality and aim to transform gender relations.

The recommended actions that will be undertaken in the Project are outlined below:

### 7.1 Mainstream gender in clinical information, safety management and disaster risk management

Gender mainstreaming is the process of assessing the implications for women and men of any planned action. It includes identifying how women and men may be affected differently by a policy, program or project and then taking steps to ensure that both women and men benefit equally from it. Gender mainstreaming involves making sure that policies, programs and projects do not discriminate against or exclude either women or men.

### 7.2 Ensure meaningful participation of women

Gender equality is achieved when women and men enjoy equal opportunities, rights, responsibilities and power in all spheres of life. This includes the elimination of discrimination based on sex and the promotion of gender equality. The Project will support full and effective participation of women and gender equality in decision-making processes at all levels. It will also promote the empowerment of women and their ability to participate fully in society.

The Project will ensure that women have equal access to health care services, including reproductive health services, and that they are able to make informed decisions about their own bodies and lives. The Project will also support the promotion of gender equality in the workplace and in the community.

The Project will promote the empowerment of women by providing them with the skills and knowledge they need to participate fully in society and to take on leadership roles. This includes providing women with access to education, training, and employment opportunities, as well as promoting their participation in decision-making processes at all levels.


---
**Page 163**
---

### National levels

For example, in sector transforming activities and development of the National Action Plan on Gender Equality, the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and Social Protection has played a leading role. The Ministry and other relevant institutions have been actively engaged in gender mainstreaming that goes beyond the listed national priorities.

Participatory approaches include programs to ensure transformative change in gender relations at all levels. For example, the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and Social Protection has worked with local communities to promote women’s rights and empowerment. Workshops are held at the community level to raise awareness about the importance of gender equality and to support the development of action plans for gender mainstreaming. The workshops also provide a platform for women to discuss their needs and priorities, which helps to identify areas where further action is needed.

### 7.3 Gender differences in health, being pregnant and consultation

Gender differences need to be properly understood and disseminated so that appropriate care can be provided. This includes the need to understand the different ways in which men and women experience illness and disease. For example, women and marginalized groups may not receive adequate care for diseases that are more common among them. Women may also face barriers to accessing health services due to cultural norms or lack of transportation.

Gender differences can also affect how people seek help and what kind of help they need. For example, men may be less likely to seek help for mental health problems than women. Similarly, marginalized groups such as LGBTQ individuals may face additional barriers to accessing healthcare.

Developing a gender-responsive consultation process is essential to ensure the involvement of different stakeholders in decision-making processes. This includes involving community members and organizations that work with marginalized groups. It also involves ensuring that consultations are inclusive and that they take into account different types of knowledge and an array of experiences.

Engaging with local leaders as well as communities can help ensure the communication of cultural and social norms that may affect health-seeking behaviors. This includes women’s groups and networks as community champions to disseminate information and promote healthy practices. It also involves developing strategies to utilize traditional knowledge of subsistence women and men (who are often easily accessible).

The local resource mobilization strategy should identify champions and traditional leaders who can help raise awareness about the importance of gender equality in health care. This includes working with community leaders to promote gender-sensitive health services and to ensure that they are responsive to the needs of all members of the community.


---
**Page 164**
---

# 7.4 Tailor preparedness and response actions to the differential needs and capacities of women

Women in low- and middle-income countries are particularly vulnerable as they work in sectors that overlook the health but underpin the economy. Women are more likely to be poor, uneducated, and living in rural areas with limited access to healthcare services. They are also more likely to be affected by poverty, conflict, and natural disasters. This has been exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic.

## 7.4.1 Address women's needs

- Ensure that preparedness and response actions address the specific needs of women.
- Integrate gender into all aspects of disaster risk reduction (DRR) planning and implementation.
- Provide adequate and safe spaces for women to participate in decision-making processes.
- Ensure that education campaigns target the specific needs and concerns of different gender groups.

## 7.4.2 Improve preparedness and response plans

- Analyze gender-differentiated responses to previous disasters and incorporate gender-sensitive measures into preparedness and response plans.
- Engage with women's rights to develop an inclusive capacity-building intervention.
- Implement a comprehensive early warning system (EWS) in collaboration with local communities, especially for vulnerable groups such as women and children.
- Ensure that financial and technical assistance are gender-responsive and that women and men have equal access to EWS resources.

## 7.4.3 Promote economic empowerment and access to resources

Economic development at all levels supports the development of adaptive capacity and climate resilience that benefits both women and men. Evidence shows that women's inclusion in decision-making processes improves outcomes for their families and communities. As a result, investment in women's economic empowerment is critical.

- Provide equal access to education, training, and resources.
- Encourage women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels.
- Promote women's leadership and entrepreneurship.
- Ensure that women have the skills and knowledge needed to participate fully in the economy.

## 7.4.4 Address specific needs

Women face unique challenges in accessing healthcare services, including reproductive health care. Women are more likely to be poor, uneducated, and living in rural areas with limited access to healthcare services. They are also more likely to be affected by poverty, conflict, and natural disasters.

- **Natural capital**: Promote access to and ownership of land and achieve equitable management of decision-making processes.
- **Social capital**: Cultivate and strengthen social networks and groups that promote mutual support and cooperation among women.
- **Human capital**: Ensure equal access to information, transport, and communication assets.
- **Financial capital**: Provide women's livelihoods through securing access to water, irrigation, and productive resources.

Women should be empowered to participate fully in the economy and have access to the resources they need to succeed. This includes providing them with the skills and knowledge needed to participate fully in the economy and have access to the resources they need to succeed.


---
**Page 165**
---

# Minimize the Risk of Gender-Based Violence

The risk of increasing gender-based violence (GBV) has been identified through existing a greater need for prevention and response to GBV in humanitarian settings. This is particularly relevant during and after disasters when women and girls are at higher risk of sexual and gender-based violence, including rape, sexual assault, domestic violence, trafficking, and forced prostitution and exploitation by armed groups. Where possible, the project will work with existing structures and protection actors in the affected areas to minimize risks.

The Project should follow Good Practices as outlined by the EU-Aided Global Crisis Support Mechanism:

1. **Risk Knowledge**: Risk assessments should include close questions on what gender-related factors contribute to specific groups of women, men, girls and boys being at greater risk during disasters.
2. **Monitoring and Warning Service**: The Project should assess which specific gender groups have been affected by the disaster and how they can be reached. Early warning information is key for early warning signs of impending crisis.
3. **Dissemination and Communication**: Gender and age differences related to communication of risks, warnings and response options need to be considered in order to reach all groups.
4. **Preparedness and Response Capabilities**: Women and children should be consulted as active participants in disaster risk reduction (DRR) planning and preparedness at the community level. They should be empowered with skills to identify early signs of different types of crisis and the post-crisis recovery period.

---

Source: [01/04/2016] Annual Report, Gender Treatment Early Warning Systems, Experiences from Nepal and Peru

# Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) forms the basis for operating the results and recommendations of the Gender Assessment presented in the preceding section. The GAP is designed to ensure that gender equality is integrated throughout the Project lifecycle, from planning through implementation and evaluation. It aims to promote the development, respect, and observance of the Great Circle UN-SGP principles and guidelines on gender equality and environmental sustainability. Where possible, it also identifies opportunities for collaboration with other projects and initiatives within the UNDP for mainstreaming gender equity and empowering gender equality.

The GAP is closely aligned to the outputs of the Project logical framework and proposes actions. The GAP constitutes the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and Gender Action Plan (GAP). It will be reviewed annually by the Project Management Team, and the Gender Action Plan will be integrated into these processes by conducting the Gender Assessment and reviewing the Action Plan.

## Promotion of Gender Equality across the Project

The Project will promote gender equality through the following actions:

- **Gender Mainstreaming**: Integrate gender considerations in all aspects of planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.
- **Gender Analysis**: Conduct a gender analysis to identify gender issues and opportunities for addressing them.
- **Gender Training**: Provide training on gender mainstreaming to project staff.

The Gender Expert will work with the Project Management Team (PMT) to ensure that gender equality is fully incorporated throughout the Project lifecycle. The Gender Expert will also provide technical support and capacity-building activities related to gender equality.

In addition, as an Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project, UNDP will provide an advisory and quality assurance role throughout the Project implementation. UNDP will ensure that the Project integrates gender equality principles into its design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation processes. This includes incorporating gender equality considerations in all aspects of the Project cycle.

## Inputs/Stakeholder Consultations

If feasible, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) will conduct an initial consultative exercise involving with women including representatives from local communities (such as youth, elderly people, women and people with disabilities), which will help to inform the gender equality objectives and actions of the Project.

UNDP - United Nations Development Programme

# consultations conducted in development of the proposed Project

The workshop will primarily focus on engagement with community level users of climate services and information, including women’s groups, youth groups, local government organizations and local communities.

The scope of the in-depth stakeholder consultation will include:
- Identification of the needs of women’s access to climate services (i.e. financial, natural resources, working capital, and support networks)
- Examination of the roles of women in the Climate Framework for Climate Services priority areas – agricultural production systems, water management, disaster risk reduction, and health
- Understanding of the barriers that hinder women from fully participating in climate services and decision-making processes
- Identification of opportunities to strengthen the enabling environment for greater mainstreaming of national service providers who are involved in the Project

Identify opportunities to strengthen the enabling environment for greater mainstreaming of national service providers who are involved in the Project.
Examine the gender roles of women and men in suitable areas where the Project will be implemented, these areas and new resources including climate information services.
Engage with existing women’s groups and community networks that could support the active engagement of women and other vulnerable groups in the Project.
Using a gender analysis framework, identify how the various Project activities could provide greater inclusion in the communities, empowerment, increased awareness of rights and local actions, management participation in governance and decision-making processes, and inclusion.

Building on existing partnerships with local governments, civil society organizations, and community-based organizations that have been involved in the field previously. New models for inclusive making climate services and decision-making processes more accessible to women and other vulnerable groups.
The Gender Expert will conduct a gender analysis of the Project’s activities and provide recommendations on how to ensure greater inclusion of women and other vulnerable groups in the Project.

The Gender Expert will also conduct a gender analysis of the Project’s activities and provide recommendations on how to ensure greater inclusion of women and other vulnerable groups in the Project.

# Monitoring, Evaluation and Reporting

The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) plan for Phase I will be refined on the basis of the experience (phase IV depth stakeholder consultations to design gender responsive M&E systems). The M&E system will be designed to ensure that it is sensitive to gender issues and includes indicators for achieving the CAP and Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) targets, and facilitating adaptive management as required to achieve the goals. It will also include a gender-responsive monitoring framework.

The M&E plan will be implemented through the following key activities:

- **Annual Performance Reports** and **Mid-Term and Term Evaluation**: These will be incorporated into the Annual Performance Reports and Mid-Term and Term Evaluations. Indicators to quantify the achievement of the project objectives will be developed, including gender-specific indicators. This will ensure that the project is on track to achieve its objectives and targets, enabling timely adjustments by the Project, and participation in learning and capacity building interventions. Monitoring will also be conducted by the Gender Expert.

- **Gender Mainstreaming Approach**: The plan will include a general mainstreaming approach through development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation to ensure that the project is gender-responsive.

  - **The Constitution of the Gender Committee (Hippo Law)**: which promotes gender disbursement in the products of grants (female land titles and inheritance).

  - **Gender-Based Violence (GBV) Prevention Plan (2017-2025)**: which includes the following strategies for achieving gender equality goals: 8 community mobilization activities, 4 government policies, awareness and advocacy campaigns, and 6 capacity-building initiatives. Gender-based violence policies and laws of Rwanda will be promoted to ensure that women have equal rights in land ownership and inheritance. The plan also aims to promote the rights of women and girls through the promotion of gender equality.

  - **Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) Strategy**: which promotes activities to reduce discrimination against women, including promoting economic empowerment of women (Duties and Responsibilities).

  - **Gender-Based Violence (GBV) Prevention Plan (2017-2025)**: which includes the following strategies for achieving gender equality goals: 8 community mobilization activities, 4 government policies, awareness and advocacy campaigns, and 6 capacity-building initiatives. Gender-based violence policies and laws of Rwanda will be promoted to ensure that women have equal rights in land ownership and inheritance. The plan also aims to promote the rights of women and girls through the promotion of gender equality.

# Stakeholder Response Mechanism

UNF's Environmental and Social Safeguards Framework mandates a Stakeholder Response Mechanism (SRM), which provides the opportunity for affected communities to raise concerns about safeguard violations and seek redress through the procedure for the SRM in detail. These procedures aim:

- What can submit a complaint
  - Who can submit a complaint
    - Individuals or groups
  - What information should be included in a complaint
    - Name, address, contact details of the complainant
    - Description of the safeguard violation and facts
    - Explanation regarding engagement with Project-affected communities and status updates
  - The SRM's mandate to help communicate and propose a resolution

The SRM serves as a complementary mechanism; the local Governance Response Mechanism (GRM) is established for the Project. The Project Coordination Agreement (PCA) with the national Extracting Entities (EE) will require that EE puts in place a functional GRM, including enhanced communication and reporting procedures. This will ensure that the SRM and GRM are complementary mechanisms to address safeguard concerns. The local community will be engaged through the GRM, which will also manage grievances with respect to environmental and social impacts of the Project. The EE will ensure that the SRM is implemented in accordance with the PCA and the UNF's safeguards policies.

## Get a Better Vision

The Global Expert who arranged this will be responsible for ensuring a gender-responsive approach to the development of early warning systems (EWS), the project, which can monitor the use of gender-based violence. The Gender Expert can conduct a long-term gender-based violence monitoring system that is integrated into the EWS and the Project's safeguards framework. This will ensure that the Project's safeguards framework includes gender-responsive components to address gender-based violence concerns who have been standing their expertise in addressing gender-based violence—including the local Foundation, FOXLAPEME ALLA, and local NGOs.

# EWS Standard

## Organisations participating in EWS working group

### EWS Working Group (EWG)

The EWG is a multi-stakeholder group that includes representatives from:

- The GDCN by the Gender Expert
- Local organisations and implementing partners
- Representatives of women and girls' groups
- Representatives of local authorities
- Representatives of other relevant stakeholders

### Tasks of the EWS Working Group

1. **Contact an in-depth, gender-sensitive consultation with all stakeholders** to:
   - Identify needs and priorities for project implementation.
   - Provide recommendations on how to address these needs and priorities.

2. **Project implementation**:
   - Ensure that the project is implemented in a way that addresses the identified needs and priorities of women and girls' groups.
   - Ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the recommendations made by the EWG.

3. **Annual working sessions** to:
   - Review progress on the implementation of the project.
   - Identify any challenges or obstacles faced by stakeholders.
   - Provide recommendations for addressing these challenges and obstacles.

4. **Targeting and monitoring**:
   - Ensure that all activities are targeted at women, girls, and other vulnerable groups.
   - Monitor and evaluate the impact of the project on these target groups.

### Type 1

| PMU | Local Consultant | Implementation Lead |
| --- | ---------------- | ------------------- |

# Gender Equality and Empowerment of Women

## Activity
- Take the lead to ensure the implementation of the project.

## Sub-activity
- Ensure the integration of gender equality into the project's activities.

## Mainstreaming Gender Equality
- Integrate gender equality in all project activities.
- Ensure that gender equality is considered in all project components and activities.

## Indicators, Baselines and Targets
- Number of gender-responsive actions developed in collaboration with women's groups and promotion of women's participation.
- Community-based EWS is responsive to gender needs.
- Awareness campaigns and training sessions on gender-responsive actions.

## Timeline
- Years 1: Co-develop and gender-responsive environmental management systems, enhance community-based early warning systems.
- Years 2: Disseminate gender-responsive early warning actions.

## Responsibility
- PMU (Project Management Unit)
- CVTL (Community Vulnerability and Thresholds Initiative)
- FSD (Financial Services Department)
- MAFF (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry)

## Estimated Budget
- USD 29,200 for co-developing gender-responsive environmental management systems.
- No additional budget required for enhancing community-based early warning systems.
- Integrated in existing budget of USD 204,850 for disseminating gender-responsive early warning actions.

# Appendix 1 - Gender Consultation Workshop in Timor-Leste™

![Timor-Leste](image-url)

## 23 November 2017

### Preparatory session to the Legal Municipality

A preparatory session was held to produce a community-based gender consultation. This methodology was chosen based on the variety of landscapes from coastal to upland areas and the diversity of languages spoken in Timor-Leste.

- **M. Leonardo Fernandes** (Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries), **M. Maria Goretti** (National Directorate for Meteorology and Geophysics), **M. Maria Goretti** (National Consultant) and **Ms. Zelia A. Martins** (National Director for Gender Change).

The session was held in the community of Liquica, which is a coastal municipality with a diverse landscape. The session identified areas with potential livelihood hazards that affect women and men differently. The session also provided a vision of the challenges to the following stage.

## 24 November 2017

### Workshop in the Urban Office

The group continued with Ms. Krishna (Gender Specialist), Ms. Zelia A. Martins (National Director for Gender Change) and Ms. Maria Goretti (National Consultant). The session was held at the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries in Dili, Timor-Leste.

The workshop was organized with **Ms. Isabel Pereira** (Directorate General for International Cooperation - DGZI) and **Dr. Lorenzo Xhani** (FAO).

![Liquica](image-url)


---
**Page 184**
---

# Background

Ullens village is located near the coast and is 30 minutes by track from the capital Dili. The village is surrounded by mountains and has a beautiful view of the sea. The majority of the population are farmers who grow rice and vegetables. The village was established in 1984 and has a population estimated to be around 200 and Catholic by religion. The village area is flat and easy to cultivate.

A number of senior women attended present for the question and answer session but yet to attend another training on gender equality. They were asked several questions such as:

- Who has water?
- Who has land?
- Who benefits?

### Why does what?

Women and men consider themselves more than farmers, although some farming activities are also normal. Division of labour is defined between men and women. Men do most of the heavy work in agriculture while women do light work. Women are responsible for cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, fetching water, and caring for children. They also prepare food for planting, as well as the school gardening program. The women also produce handicrafts such as mats, bags, and other products. Men are involved in the husbandry of animals such as pigs, buffaloes, goats, cows, and chickens.


---
**Page 185**
---

Some of the animals are used for the diary and so are considered of higher value than the farm or garden produce. The animals are also a source of manure which is used to fertilize the gardens and fields. Manure is essential in maintaining the fertility of the soil, and without it, the community would not be able to grow enough food to sustain itself. Women spend at least 12 hours a day carrying out domestic chores such as cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, fetching water, and caring for children.

Feeding the community a major task in the area, although drought stricken areas are in a worse condition than others. The women have to walk long distances to fetch water from the river or well. Based on the interview and observation, it is evident that the women have to carry water from the river or well using buckets. This is a time-consuming process as they have to make several trips to collect enough water for their families.

Based on the interview and observation, it is evident that the women have to carry water from the river or well using buckets. This is a time-consuming process as they have to make several trips to collect enough water for their families. The men are responsible for ploughing the fields and harvesting crops. They also help with the construction of houses and other buildings in the community.

Disasters such as floods do not only destroy the community, the man in the village have also contributed to the destruction since they draw water from the river. The work to construct stone walls that would prevent soil erosion is a major task for the community but economic constraints have limited their ability to carry out this task.

The women are responsible for the construction of the houses and other buildings in the community. They use locally available materials such as mud, wood, and grass to build the houses. The men are responsible for the construction of the stone walls that would prevent soil erosion. The work is done by hand and it is a time-consuming process.

**Page 186**
---

who have access to traditional knowledge about the land and resources on the land. Women do not have

the same access as men.

A household that owns an animal (UBD 10) - UBD 10 is paid twice. The household would spend money

on food, clothing, and other things. The household would also use the money to pay for school fees and

to produce their own food. The household would also use the money to buy a radio or TV.

Men often have more access to make phone calls. Some households have access to TV and

radio, but very few read the newspaper. The most preferred channel to reach women is TV.

Women are not involved in decision-making processes at the community level. Women do not

have access to communication devices. It is expensive to own one.

Who decides?
Men decide on household materials, assets and finances. When they have tried direct influence in

the household, it has been through their wives. Men make decisions for the household, whether men or women. The decision is to reach an agreement or

to solve a problem. Women are not involved in decision-making.

Who benefits?
The following needs with regards to climate impacts were expressed by men, women, young people and

the elderly:

Men:
- More food (two days) to save household.
- Access to water for early warning and
  - Early warning systems.
Women:
- At least two days lead time to save household.
- Access to information on climate change.
- Enough fuel to cook when there is no rain, and
  - Early warning systems.
Young people:
- Access to the best information and
  - Early warning systems for better responses.
People with a disability:
- Have good time to save food,
- Access to early warning systems and
  - Early warning systems for people with disabilities.

For an effective early warning system for households, both men and women consider saving lives as their

primary goal. Secondly is the need concerning saving animals, as important, and some consider saving

property. The most important thing is to save lives. The community should be involved in the process of

early warning systems. No special mention was made about the preferences of young girls, although it is clear that girls would be


---
**Page 187**
---

more vulnerable in floods than boys. Furthermore, for an effective early warning system for floods in the country to be better to meet their specific needs. Training is also considered essential for men, women, youths and children. The training should include the roles of community leaders as champions is also considered important by women, who also value person-person contact in disaster preparedness.
